Ahead of its IPO filing, Anthropic pledges to prioritize humanity’s benefit. Here’s how

📅 Nov 16, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

When Anthropic goes public on Wall Street, the new pressure to deliver profits will test a commitment it has made since its founding: to prioritize humanity’s benefit as it develops artificial intelligence.

The San Francisco startup, which plans an initial public offering this month, has sketched out a governance structure that it says will protect the company’s broader mission against the pull of financial interests. But the mechanisms it describes give the company wide leeway to decide how to measure whether it’s benefiting society, experts say.

Like its rival OpenAI, Anthropic is a public benefit corporation, a legal designation used by companies that pledge to balance shareholder interests with the public good. How that will play out for companies worth potentially trillions of dollars is uncharted territory.

“I think then the real test emerges if and when the question of public benefit becomes costly” — or when the public benefit comes into conflict with making a profit, said Sarah Kreps, a professor and director of Cornell University’s Tech Policy Institute.

In its regulatory filings, Anthropic pledges to pursue long-term benefits of humanity through the responsible development of advanced AI. “We strive to help bring humanity safely into an era where transformative AI exerts tremendous and potentially decisive influence over a broad range of human undertakings,” the company said. Anthropic also has pledged at least 5% of its stock to a “public benefit” fund and its founders have committed to donate 80% of their wealth.

A pledge to do good is baked into corporate structure

Other tech companies have pledged to do good including Google, which prominently featured its then-motto, “Don’t Be Evil,” in its investor prospectus when it went public in 2004. But the words were not legally binding, and critics have accused the tech giant of leaving them behind.

Elon Musk’s takeover of Twitter in 2021 shows how the conflict between profit and public good might play out in real life, said Stavros Gadinis, a law professor at UC Berkeley School of Law.

“Let’s say that Twitter’s directors felt that Elon was the wrong guy to police the public square because they didn’t trust him, they didn’t like him, they didn’t think he’d be fair, whatever it was,” Gadinis said. “But he made them an offer that was so high compared to what their advisors told them the company was really worth that it would be against their fiduciary duties to not sell to Elon. This is the situation that the public benefit charter is trying to avoid.”

Had Twitter been a public benefit corporation, it could have been required to consider factors beyond maximizing shareholder value when selling the company.

There aren’t many public benefit corporations whose shares are publicly traded and they are all much smaller than Anthropic and OpenAI. They include the eyewear company Warby Parker, the insurance company Lemonade and software developer Veeva Systems, which had a conventional corporate structure when it went public in 2013 and converted to a public benefit corporation in 2021.

Anthropic’s idealistic ambitions for how it develops AI could run up against competitive pressure, such as from China, Kreps said.

Anthropic and OpenAI’s models have gone from answering almost any question, even if it was dangerous, to becoming much more circumspect. But as they face more competition, including from open-source Chinese models that likely will tell you whatever you want, she said, “will they start to rethink some of those guardrails? And what happens then?”

The setup generally is not preferred by shareholders because it gives more power to the company’s board than it does to shareholders.

Whatever a company decides as its public benefit mission, it will likely be much more difficult to measure than profits. That’s particularly true for AI companies, whose leaders have exalted AI as the solution to many of humanity’s problems — from curing cancer to creating economic prosperity.

“There are these echo chambers, the Silicon Valley bubble where they really think that what they’re doing is for the good of humanity,” Kreps said. And someone immersed in that environment, she added, might not even ask the right questions or understand how “public benefit” should be measured.

A trust aims to hold Anthropic accountable

A Long-Term Benefit Trust created by Anthropic is intended to operate as an independent body of trustees with expertise in AI safety, national security, public policy, and social enterprise.

Through a special Class T common stock, the trust “generally has the authority to elect and remove” Class T directors, who together will constitute a majority of Anthropic’s board of directors, according to the IPO filing. The Class T common stock is a single share that allows the five trustees to vote as a unit. It has no economic rights — meaning they get to vote but they don’t see any financial benefit or losses from the share and can’t sell it.

“The LTBT’s arrangements are designed to minimize the LTBT’s and the Trustees’ exposure to our financial performance and grant the Trustees sufficient independence to ensure we are striking this balance appropriately,” the filing said.

Three of the company’s board directors are elected by the trust directly, and another three are elected by other shareholders, which include the company founders with a 50.1% voting majority. A seventh director, with a potentially tie-breaking vote, is nominated by the trust and the holders of the Class T shares, but cannot be appointed or removed without consent from the board’s nominating committee, which is expected to be controlled by the founders.

Effectively, the trust can only hold Anthropic accountable on issues such as AI safety and the company’s broader mission up to a point, Gadinis said.

The trust “cannot determine the everyday decisions of these directors or the management of the company,” he said. “If they disagree, they will have to go through the effort of removing directors, and they may not be able to remove a majority.”

As a public company, Anthropic also will be required to report finances and generally be more transparent than a privately held one. Many public companies dealing with AI, including Google and Microsoft, regularly update the public on efforts to develop AI responsibly.

The incentive for transparency could be as powerful as other mechanisms set up in Anthropic’s corporate structure, Gadinis said.

“I do think that by being public, we are going to get a lot more information, because a lot of people are going to be asking questions, and companies will be responding to these questions. not only because they’re required by security laws, but also because they want to build this kind of trust with the investing community,” he said.

—Barbara Ortutay, AP Technology Writer

Crook turns free trials into phishing factory, as LearnWorlds sends 13,000 fake YouTube copyright notices

📅 Nov 16, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

570 bogus accounts, one rewritten welcome email, and a fake YouTube page, but CEO says no breach

LearnWorlds, a software and learning course platform, says an attacker sent 13,000 phishing emails to customers over seven days by using automated tools to open free trial accounts. The company insists its systems were not breached.

Panos Siozos, Co-founder and CEO at LearnWorlds, said the miscreant created around 570 free trial accounts on the platform between November 7-14, which they used to issue fake copyright infringement notices to customers.

"We have found no evidence of unauthorized access to existing customer schools or personal data held for our customers and their end users," he told The Register.

According to Siozos, the attacker bypassed the platform's per-customer limit on trial subscriptions and exploited its welcome email feature to send the phishing emails.

"In each trial they added their own list of target addresses and rewrote the standard welcome email as a fake copyright notice," he said. "Adding those users triggered the welcome email from our sending address."

The first email was sent on November 7, after which the company detected the suspicious signups and blocked the first sender on November 9, although the attacker returned to send a second wave of emails within 40 minutes.

LearnWorlds removed all the trial accounts by November 10 and one paid account, which was used to launch more emails, on November 14.

"In total, around 13,000 emails were sent to addresses on external recipient lists supplied by the sender," said Siozos. "The trial accounts sent 9,853 between November 7-9, and the paid account sent 3,723 on November 14.

"We can’t tell how many people clicked, because the link was disguised to hide clicks. The recipient addresses were the sender’s own list."

The CEO confirmed that LearnWorlds has since enhanced its bot checks and is placing holds on new accounts if their emails trigger fraud flags.

"We have also examined the novel patterns the attackers used to create more advanced scoring mechanisms. We have reported the phishing website so it can be taken down. Further measures are currently being examined.

"Anyone who signed in on the fake page should change their Google password and review their account’s recent security activity."

The company's confirmation follows suspicions of foul play aired by users on social media. User reports included copies of the emails they received, which demanded urgent responses to fake copyright infringement claims related to YouTube content.

Among the more prominent recipients of the copyright infringement email notices was Marcus Hutchins, of WannaCry fame, who claimed he received an email from the company’s official mail server.

The email, a copy of which Hutchins posted to X, referenced general intellectual property and copyright infringements but failed to specify any specific issues. The email prompted the recipient to click a YouTube link to see the “supporting documentation submitted by LearnWorks.”

The link predictably turned out to be a YouTube redirect link, taking recipients to a separate website that presents them with a real Google OAuth page. If the user completed the authorization flow, it would have handed attackers control of the target’s Google and YouTube accounts.

“The site is designed to look like an official YouTube webpage that enables users to review copyright claims against their channel,” Hutchins wrote. “It contains both the YouTube and Google branding, as well as a very similar theme to the official YouTube one.”

According to Hutchins' screenshots, and those posted by others, the attacker was actively tweaking the email style and format, although the underlying lure and authentication mechanisms remained the same.

Hutchins didn't specify exactly how the phishing page steals access to targets’ Google accounts, although his description aligns with the steps often seen in OAuth consent phishing attacks.

These involve malicious websites serving victims genuine OAuth pages, into which unwitting victims enter their credentials and pass the relevant MFA tests. Typical scenarios see permission pages appear immediately after, and if the user accepts, the malicious website is handed OAuth access tokens to the victim’s account, effectively the keys to control it.

LearnWorlds, based in Cyprus, claims to have two million active monthly learners on its platform accessing 900,000 courses and more than 14,000 business customers. Since launching in 2014, it claims to have generated more than $1 billion in revenue.

The business focuses on white-label courses - training programs developed by one business and sold to another, assuming the buyer will change the branding and present the course materials as their own. ®

OpenAI will embed Apollo researchers to investigate AI subversion

📅 Nov 16, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

OpenAI will embed six Apollo Research safety evaluators in its organization as part of a pilot, Apollo said Monday. The news follows a series of safety incidents, including one in which an OpenAI agent swarm broke out of its sandbox and hacked into Hugging Face during cybersecurity testing. Many AI safety researchers, including from Apollo, have argued such an event might have been avoided if outside evaluators had been embedded within OpenAI to monitor its safety testing.

Apollo did not share when the pilot will begin. TechCrunch has reached out to OpenAI for comment.

The idea of embedding evaluators into frontier AI labs first came from Anthropic, but OpenAI appears to be the first to take action. Until recently, AI companies have only brought in outside reviewers to test models after post-training and shortly before public release. Evaluators say this is insufficient because it doesn’t allow them to test for misaligned behaviors like scheming during training or to monitor internal deployment use cases that could still pose a threat.

The pilot with OpenAI and Apollo will focus on investigating whether models attempt to subvert their own or their successors’ alignment or control mechanisms. In other words, they’ll be on the lookout for whether AI models try to undermine the safety rules and safeguards designed to keep them, or future versions of themselves, under human control. OpenAI recently published findings that while training GPT-5.6 Sol, it began leaving instructions for future versions of itself telling them to conceal mistakes and misaligned behavior from the user.

Per Apollo, the pilot will examine whether training incentivizes subversion; whether evaluations can elicit subversion; whether subversion occurs during internal deployment; and whether reasoning relevant to subversion would be detectable. These risks are becoming increasingly important to monitor as AI models grow more capable of acting autonomously and deceiving humans. Apollo’s previous research with OpenAI found that models can behave differently when they know they’re being tested and tend to prioritize what evaluators want to see over users’ actual institutions.

Many in the AI safety community have warned that AI labs might invite try to stymy evaluators’ independence, especially if outsiders find unflattering evidence that labs would rather keep hidden. Apollo says an essential part of the pilot is OpenAI’s agreement that the firm can publicly explain the conditions and limitations of its work.

Still, there are areas where Apollo believes its access to OpenAI’s systems is lacking. For a truly independent evaluation of the company’s AI models, Apollo says it needs true employee-level access to OpenAI’s systems, including “all data, records and documentation relevant to their assessment even if a developer was trying to hide unfavorable information.” Getting access to all data, including training data and evaluation logs which companies are loathe to show to outsiders, is important for, say, checking a company’s claims about how a model performed or determining when certain concerning behavior emerged.

In previous coverage, John Steidley, chief of staff at Palisade Research, told TechCrunch that it’s important to see this data in order to assess whether a model was trained to perform well on a “shutdown resistance benchmark.”

In OpenAI’s first pilot, the AI lab is providing Apollo with access to only the information required to assess whether the models engage in subversion attempts. This includes “relevant training code and datasets, reinforcement-learning rollouts with available reasoning traces and rewards, and the ability to query intermediate model checkpoints.”

The scope of access will be limited to “only the agreed claims and only for the specific models and time periods agreed for the assessment,” Apollo says. OpenAI still reserves the right to withhold certain information within that scope if it deems it necessary because of technical infeasibility, security risks, or IP protection. The company agreed not to withhold information simply because it is unfavorable.

Apollo also won’t get access to all OpenAI staffers, instead being limited to “only authorized staff,” which are participants that the AI lab selects, though Apollo can suggest certain individuals. Crucially, if an OpenAI employee outside this group raises confidential information within the scope of Apollo’s research, the firm cannot follow up and will need to cease communication and route followup through OpenAI.

That condition is significant. It comes in the wake of OpenAI firing three researchers for allegedly sharing confidential information with safety evaluation firm METR — which performed the Hugging Face incident post-mortem alongside Redwood.

The pilot will also be limited in how much researchers can disclose. While Apollo will be allowed to publish independent conclusions of its findings, OpenAI will be able to limit evidence available to substantiate them publicly, which could make it hard for outsiders to independently assess the findings. Apollo has pushed back against this limitation, saying believes researchers should be allowed to disclose the findings of the evaluation “to an appropriate outside audience even if the developer was trying to prevent the disclosure of unfavorable findings.”

While OpenAI is not granting all of Apollo’s conditions for a truly independent evaluation, the firm says the pilot is “an ambitious first step towards deeper access and disclosure rights,” and that it is working towards running full embedded evaluations with multiple AI developers in the future.

Fed Chairman Kevin Warsh stresses importance of AI to economy, but says it could ‘disappoint’

📅 2026-11-16  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

[The stream is slated to start at noon ET. Please refresh the page if you do not see a player above.]

Federal Reserve Chairman Kevin Warsh on Monday highlighted the potential economic benefits of artificial intelligence while warning that the emerging technology also could lead to widespread job losses or fail to deliver on its lofty expectations.

Speaking in San Francisco before a blue-ribbon task force he assembled to examine productivity and employment, the central bank leader stressed that AI "will have significant impact on the economy, with important implications for monetary policy down the road."

"I stress that while I'm not here to offer predictions, I'm not exactly impartial about American leadership in a time so filled with both challenge and possibility," he said in prepared remarks before his productivity and jobs task force.

In his remarks, Warsh used the term "super intelligence" instead of artificial intelligence and described it as "an entirely new force in economic life and society" that has already begun to transform economic activity. President Donald Trump recently ordered that the U.S. government now use the term "super intelligence" almost exclusively instead of AI.

Since taking the Fed chair position, Warsh has emphasized the importance of AI and its potential to improve productivity and ultimately to be a disinflationary force on the economy.

In his remarks Monday, he noted that AI could lead to productivity gains that could accelerate real economic growth beyond its current range while keeping employment near full capacity. On the downside, he said "less positive" outcomes include job displacement through automation that could raise unemployment.

At the same time, he outlined a third scenario where AI could simply "disappoint."

"After significant capital investment and stock market gains, expected returns could turn down and equity valuations could fall, leading to an economic slowdown and a more notable rise in unemployment," he said. "In time, the economy would recover, but without the productivity gains."

The potential for AI advancement has driven stock market valuations higher and led to companies raising capital to continue competing.

Monday's conference features discussion panels with business leaders, economists and academics. The final session will be a fireside chat that will include Warsh and task force leader and AI advocate Marc Andreessen, co-founder of Andreessen Horowitz.

Warsh created four other task forces addressing topics such as Fed communication, data reliance, balance sheet policy and inflation frameworks.

The chairman noted that this was the first visit he has made to a regional bank.

The Leonid meteor shower peaks Nov. 17-18! Here's what you need to know

📅 Nov 16, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Join The Club for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation, and sign you up to our newsletter.

Latest space missions, discoveries and exploration updates.

Stargazing tips, cosmic events and expert insights on the universe

Space news, cosmic updates and expert insights straight to your inbox.

Breaking space news, the latest updates on rocket launches, skywatching events and more!

Breaking space news, the latest updates on rocket launches, skywatching events and more!

Sign up to our monthly entertainment newsletter to keep up with all our coverage of the latest sci-fi and space movies, tv shows, games and books.

Discover this week's must-see night sky events, moon phases, and stunning astrophotos. Sign up for our skywatching newsletter and explore the universe with us!

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

The Leonid meteor shower peaks overnight on Nov. 17-18, sending shooting stars racing across the night sky from the direction of the constellation Leo.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

The Leonid meteor shower peaks Nov. 17-18 sending shooting stars and fireballs across the autumn sky as Earth passes through a stream of debris left by Comet 55P/Tempel-Tuttle.

Up to 15 meteors could be spotted per hour under perfect dark sky conditions on the night of Nov. 17-18, according to the International Meteor Organisation. NASA, meanwhile, has given a more conservative rate of less than three meteors per hour — time will tell.

Leonid meteors appear to race away from a point of origin, or radiant, in the constellation Leo, which glistens high above the southeastern horizon in the hours before dawn in mid-November. Both Jupiter and Mars will be separated by just a few degrees in the constellation Leo on the night of the peak, making it particularly easy to find in 2026!

The best time to spot Leonids is in the hours before dawn on Nov. 18, when the radiant will reach its highest point in the southeastern sky, maximizing the amount of meteors that may be seen skimming through Earth's atmosphere. By then, the waxing moon will have set, leaving darker skies and a better chance of spotting fainter meteors.

Shooting stars that appear close to the radiant will strike Earth almost head-on, leaving the shortest trails. Instead, find a patch of sky a short distance away from the constellation Leo, where the meteors will be at their most impressive, and give your eyes 20-30 minutes to fully adapt to the dark.

As if shooting stars weren't enough, the Leonid shower is also known for spawning dazzling fireballs and long-lasting Earth-grazing meteors, which strike our atmosphere at an oblique angle, leaving long trains as they skim through before passing back out into space.

The shower is also capable of producing magnificent meteor storms when Earth passes through particularly dense trails of debris left behind by Comet 55P/Tempel-Tuttle, which orbits the sun approximately every 33 years. Sadly, we'll have to wait a bit for the next big show. According to the Planetary Society, another Leonid meteor storm could occur as early as 2031, while a study released in August 2026 predicts potentially spectacular displays in 2033 and 2034.

Want to photograph a shooting star? Then be sure to read our guide to imaging shooting stars, along with our selections of the best lenses and cameras for astrophotography on the market in 2026.

Editor's Note: If you would like to share your Leonid photos with Space.com's readers, then please send your photo(s), comments, and your name and location to spacephotos@space.com.

Instagram Is Bringing Amazon Affiliate Links Directly to Reels and Posts

📅 Nov 16, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Instagram announced a change on Monday that may spell the end for users being nudged to visit a creator’s “link in bio.” At least when they’re promoting products sold on Amazon.

Starting today, creators and businesses can attach multiple Amazon affiliate links to their Instagram content—up to 30 per video or 20 per regular feed post. (Affiliate links let creators earn a commission on any resulting sales.) To participate in this Amazon program, creators must be US-based adults with a professional account that has over 1,000 followers.

Instagram’s parent company Meta dropped this shopping-focused change right before the holiday season really kicks off. As creators look to cash in on Amazon's sales, you might expect an avalanche of affiliate posts as you sip hot cocoa and scroll through Reels. But Instagram claims this won’t be the case.

“We don’t expect a sudden influx of shopping content from this launch,” Instagram spokesperson Megan Buckley tells WIRED. “Instagram’s recommendation systems will continue to recommend content based on people’s interests and what we think they’ll enjoy and find valuable. This update makes creators’ product recommendations easier to shop.”

For years, Instagram users could attach a single external link to their bio information. This led to the rise of bundlers like Linktree, where creators could create simple one-page sites with multiple links to content, like shopping recommendations or news articles. In 2023, Instagram expanded the number of links allowed in profiles to five. This most recent update could change how it feels to scroll Instagram, moving more shopping nudges from Amazon storefronts hidden in the bio to direct links on the viral videos.

In an example shared by Meta, a woman recording a Reel leans into her camera as she applies makeup. The caption reads: “My favorite lip tint these days!” Above the creator’s name, a small image tile pops up with the Amazon product. Tap the product and a page opens inside Instagram with the price. Tap one of the listed items, and Instagram will bump you over to the Amazon app or website to complete your purchase.

This change comes as social media shopping habits continue to expand and evolve. TikTok, one of Meta’s primary competitors, has been quite successful in expanding users’ purchasing habits through the app. Around this time last year, an analytics firm estimated that $19 billion worth of products were sold globally through TikTok Shop, putting it roughly the same size as eBay. Unlike TikTok, however, Instagram will not take a cut of the sales (at least for now). Reddit is also experimenting with how users shop heading into this holiday season, recently announcing Reddit Reviews, which uses generative AI to summarize user sentiment about products.

For over a decade, Instagram developers have searched for ways to streamline how users spend money through the app. In 2016, WIRED wrote about how the social media app added select commerce links from brands like Warby Parker, a fairly novel attempt at the time to get people to buy stuff through social media. A few years later, the Instagram experience morphed into something like a “digital mall” with in-app checkouts.

This latest attempt moves links from creator bios and places them directly in users' feeds. While this could be a boon for creators, the platform’s implementation could annoy users, who are already inundated with ads online. We’ll see if this change clicks.

5 Home Decor Trends That Make Thanksgiving Feel Warm & Welcoming

"One of the biggest misconceptions in design is that everything must match perfectly. Some of the most beautiful homes are the ones that feel like they've evolved over time rather than being purchased all at once," she shared.

Reminder, "it is less about perfection and more about creating a home that tells a story," she added.

OpenAI releases a new image classifier and ChatGPT warnings to combat CSAM

📅 Nov 16, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

OpenAI today announced new tools to combat child sexual abuse material (CSAM) amid growing safety concerns around the use of AI tools. These features include a new image classifier to detect CSAM, warnings and temporary blocks to prevent users from creating or accessing content that sexualizes children, a new guideline for developers, and a set of tools for law enforcement.

The company unveiled a new image classifier that can detect CSAM images, including the ones generated by AI. OpenAI is giving select companies access to its classifier through its API at no cost. The tool looks for visual signs of abuse in images not seen before and assigns a score that can give an indicative direction to reviewers about dealing with CSAM.

OpenAI said that it developed this classifier with the National Center for Missing and Exploited Children (NCMEC), and it is working with Tech Coalition and ROOST to share access to the tool. In its early testing with partners, the AI company found that the classifier flags 48% fewer images while finding the same number of abusive images, reducing the work of reviewers. Plus, images flagged by the classifier are twice as likely to be abusive images.

What’s more, the company is adding new warning screens in ChatGPT to prevent users from accessing material that sexualizes children. ChatGPT will show an alert to users attempting such prompts, direct users towards specialized support to stop harmful behaviour, and also suspend the account for 24 hours as a warning. The app already blocks CSAM content and requests related to it. It said that repeated offences could lead to the account being removed permanently.

OpenAI noted that it formed the message and guidelines around this behavior with organizations and researchers, including Protect Children, the Lucy Faithfull Foundation, and Joel Scanlan at the University of Tasmania, who independently evaluated a trial of deterrent messages.

The company is also publishing guidelines for developers using OpenAI tools to prevent, detect, and help users report CSAM content. It said that in early testing, the guidelines have helped a number of developers adopt child safety tooling and register to report CSAM to NCMEC. OpenAI is also equipping law enforcement dealing with child safety with AI-powered features like dedicated plugins, investigative tools, and complex data analysis, with access managed by program administrators. It is also providing training to investigators along with a field guide on how to use AI.

OpenAI has had several lawsuits against the company blaming its technology for harming users, especially children. Saftey-focused nonprofit Common Sense Media labeled OpenAI’s tech as “unacceptable risk” for teens. Other AI chatbots — specifically Grok — have been used to create nonconsensual sexualized imagery, including of children.

The AI startup had made changes in the last 12 months to its technology including releasing a safety blueprint, updating content guidelines to bar inappropritate content, retuning its model to prevent them from generating harmful content, and releasing a special version of ChatGPT for teens.

Instagram now lets creators add Amazon affiliate links in reels and feed posts

📅 Nov 16, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Instagram announced on Monday that it’s launching an integration that will give creators a new way to earn by adding Amazon affiliate links directly in reels and feed posts. Creators can add up to 30 products per reel or 20 per feed post, which makes it possible for them to earn from multiple products through one piece of content.

While creators have long shared Amazon affiliate links through stories, DMs, and their bios, the new integration brings them to new parts of the app. The Meta-owned company told TechCrunch in an email that it has seen product links directly on creators’ reels and posts draw about twice as many clicks as the links in their bio.

By allowing creators to embed multiple product links directly in Reels and feed posts, the platform is expanding their opportunities to monetize content, while also giving them more flexibility in how they share recommendations. The change also strengthens the platform’s appeal to creators, as it continues to compete with rivals like TikTok and YouTube for their attention and content.

Instagram says adding products and affiliate links is free, and that it doesn’t take a commission on purchases or charge transaction fees.

Eligible creators can now connect their Instagram and Amazon Influencer accounts through the Monetization section of their Professional Dashboard. Once connected, Instagram automatically adds affiliate links when creators tag Amazon products in their content, with links directing viewers to the Amazon Shopping app. Connected affiliates also unlock image previews for their links, making it easier for viewers to recognize the products.

When creators connect their Amazon Associates account to Instagram, affiliate attribution is automatically applied to any Amazon product they add, even when they paste a regular product link, which wasn’t the case before, as they were required to add an affiliate URL.

Instagram says creators can also see each product’s estimated commission rate before adding it to a post or reel, with commissions continuing to be paid out by Amazon.

The new integration marks another step in Instagram’s efforts to expand its monetization tools while also making affiliate marketing more accessible for creators on its platform.

Google adds 66 regional dialects to Gemini

📅 Nov 16, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The feature was teased during Google I/O earlier this year.

One of the less visible risks of conversational AI is its flattening effect on a culture's unique dialect and accent. Humanity speaks with a thousand voices, but assistants often just respond in a nicely generic transatlantic monotone. Now, Google is doing something about it by adding 66 regional dialects to Gemini to help it better relate to the people using it. The feature was first teased at Google I/O earlier this year, and starting today, the Android and iOS apps will be able to speak to users in a variety of voices from different countries.

For instance, UK users can set Gemini to speak in a Geordie, Scouse and Brummie accent to better engage with people from Tyneside, Merseyside and Birmingham. The list also includes Scots, Welsh, Northern Irish and Multicultural London English, among many others. And it's not just the UK that gets this treatment, with tailored dialects for India, Brazil, Indonesia and Korea also in the mix.

Google says it worked with native speakers to ensure the voices were authentic and clear, rather than exaggerated and stereotypical. It added that Gemini isn't going to suddenly fill its responses with "street slang," but instead focus on sounding more "like a local." Given how much pride people put into their accent as a signifier of their heritage, it'll be very interesting to see how well these voices are received by the people intended to use them.

To activate the voices, you'll need to access the voice settings in the Gemini app or tap the three-dot menu. Google suggests a good way to test their authenticity is to ask for a recommendation for local food to better hear the "warmth and rhythm of each voice."

What it will take for CMOs to reach the next frontier of AI-driven growth

📅 Nov 16, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

CMOs at the largest enterprise brands across consumer goods, financial services, B2B and more are all struggling with the same thing: more AI technology has not translated into more business growth. They’re stuck chasing efficiency gains, funding tools that optimize tasks inside functional silos, running pilots that go nowhere and delegating AI decisions to IT teams.

But a few CMOs are doing it differently. Eric Schwartz, CMO of The Clorox Company, told me his marketers are building AI systems that produce highly optimized product content. They’re encoding brand voice, compliance guardrails and seasonal context into agents that work across every brand in their portfolio. Their goal isn’t to save time on doing what human writers can already do. Instead, they want to codify, scale and orchestrate the expertise that increases relevance, engagement and conversion with consumers. That’s a growth play.

This is an operating-model decision that the CMO has to own: give marketers the strategic, system-building work and shift execution to AI agents. That takes much more than a chatbot built for a solo work experience.

“I tell everyone that tries to pitch me about AI capabilities that the last thing I need is a 14th chatbot to save our marketers time,” Schwartz said. “What I need is interoperable functionality.”

Schwartz isn’t alone. A vanguard of marketers are charting the frontier of AI-native marketing. To do this requires three fundamental shifts.

Employees are becoming more efficient with AI, but report that leaders aren’t asking them to reinvest the productivity gains into strategic work; they’re just expected to produce more output. Boston Consulting Group found that 66% of frontline employees receive limited or no guidance on using saved time, and more than half don’t reinvest it into strategic work.

Measuring AI success solely on efficiency invites efficiency’s reward: a smaller budget.

For example, one CMO’s AI-powered content system is saving the company 500 hours a month. But the company’s CFO saw the dashboard and asked: “If marketing can do this with 500 fewer hours, why does the budget stay the same?” The dashboard was supposed to prove value. Instead, it made the case for a cut.

A marketing team at a regulated financial services company chose a different approach. The team was losing 360 hours a week to legal review bottlenecks, manual multi-channel content creation and constant product updates across 800-plus references. The team built a system with compliance encoded into the workflow and distribution automated across 25 channels. The result: 360 hours freed up weekly, 90% less editing time and zero agency spend.

The marketing team reinvested those hours into producing an award-winning ad campaign, not producing more of the same content. In a category where most consumers can’t name three financial lenders, breaking through the sameness drove brand recall, pipeline and market share. The system handled the production, but the humans did the work that won.

The AI opportunity isn’t time saved. Frontier CMOs reinvest that time in architecture, curation and orchestration: designing agentic workflows, encoding brand voice and steering the system to drive growth. The humans who know best what makes their brand different turn saved time into a real advantage.

Historically, marketing and revenue have operated on separate tracks. In B2B, marketing fills the top of the funnel and hands off to sales. In consumer brands, marketing creates content while commerce manages the shelf. In financial services, brand marketing and the advisors who own the relationship rarely connect. In most organizations, no single team owns the moments that decide the sale.

Sixty-seven percent of B2B buyers now prefer a rep-free experience, according to data from Gartner. Consumers and bank customers are doing their own research too. In financial services, buyers are asking AI tools for recommendations before they ever talk to an advisor. These buyers aren’t waiting for a handoff.At the frontier, there are no handoffs. AI agents carry customer data, market signals and brand and compliance context between marketing and revenue teams in real time. Context doesn’t die at a cross-functional boundary. It compounds.

Schwartz described what this looks like at Clorox. The company’s media ecosystem spans retail media, e-commerce and traditional retail, and the goal is to connect performance back to sales — or to the signals that point to sales. Most marketing organizations stop at creating and deploying campaigns. Schwartz wants that signal flowing back to show where to lean in and scale and to shape what gets created next. By closing that loop, a media budget compounds into growth instead of disappearing.

A CMO recently told me his organization has 40 active pilots, all marked green. Everything worked in the lab, but nothing survived contact with the organization. IT loved the tools, but marketing couldn’t use them.

This happens when AI adoption is delegated to IT. The CMO sponsors the budget but doesn’t own the transformation. In Writer’s 2026 survey, more than half of CMOs (52.3%) reported “significant knowledge gaps” about AI. Gartner predicts that by 2027, lack of AI literacy will rank among the top three reasons CMOs are replaced.

At the frontier, the CMO owns the entire marketing AI agenda: selecting tools, redesigning workflows and deciding what should remain human-led. That means making AI adoption a cultural shift, not a software rollout. And that can’t be delegated.

Schwartz is an example of what this looks like in practice. He made AI adoption his own rather than delegating it to an innovation team or task force.

The CMOs making progress are changing how the work gets done, moving from traditional marketing operations toward a new model of driving growth with AI. They aren’t simply doing the same work with AI layered on top, but reimagining foundational structures, systems and strategies for a new era of marketing. The frontier isn’t a place a CMO arrives at. It’s the way a CMO operates.Partner insights from Writer

The e-commerce giant is central for many consumer brand advertisers, but measurement changes could throw a wrench into their plans for next year.

More brands are gravitating toward live shopping, a growing channel that is set to play an outsized influence on shopping trends.

A new product manager role will explore ad-related monetization, suggesting the platform could make ads a bigger part of its business.

Moncada racconta Cisse: "Lo conoscevamo bene, non è stato difficile convincere il Club. Penso che non si renda ancora conto del suo potenziale"

📅 Nov 16, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

L’ex DT del Milan Geoffrey Moncada è stato ospite del canale “Calcio e Pepe” su YouTube. Il dirigente francese ha parlato a lungo di Alphadjo Cisse, rivelazione della stagione rossonera e capocannoniere del Milan con i suoi 8 gol. Il classe 2006 è stato acquistato l’anno scorso proprio da Moncada, che poi a fine stagione ha lasciato il Milan per decisione di Cardinale insieme a Furlani, Tare ed Allegri.

Le prime partite visionate del giovane Cisse: “Avevamo uno scout che lavorava nei dintorni di Verona e ci aveva subito segnalato questo ragazzo, Alphadjo Cisse, che giocava nell'Under 17 e poi nella Primavera, nell'Under 20. Così siamo andati a vederlo ed era molto interessante, perché è un giocatore completamente diverso dai classici profili del settore giovanile italiano. Poteva giocare da seconda punta, da esterno, da mezzala, da numero 10. Praticamente a ogni partita cambiava posizione".

Sulla possibilità di portarlo subito al Milan: “Due o tre anni fa abbiamo avviato il progetto Milan Futuro. La nostra idea era quella di prenderlo per quella squadra un anno prima, ma non siamo riusciti a trovare un accordo con l'Hellas Verona. Volevano tenerlo e hanno deciso di mandarlo in prestito in Serie B, al Catanzaro Ovviamente lo abbiamo seguito molto attentamente anche lì, in Serie B, e lì è letteralmente esploso nei primi sei mesi. Ci siamo detti che aveva superato talmente bene il salto dalla Primavera e dalla panchina del Verona alla Serie B che a quel punto bisognava muoversi".

Come hai convinto il Club a puntare su di lui? "Convincere il club non è stato così difficile, perché lavoravamo su Cisse già da diversi anni. Il club lo conosceva, ne avevamo parlato, io stesso ne avevo parlato con l'amministratore delegato e con i diversi allenatori che si erano succeduti. Avevamo uno storico abbastanza importante delle sue partite e delle sue prestazioni. Insomma, conoscevamo già abbastanza bene il dossier. La difficoltà, a volte, è il tempismo. Eravamo nella finestra di mercato invernale e bisognava fare in fretta. Perché altrimenti il PSV Eindhoven lo avrebbe preso direttamente. E invece di pagare tra i 7 e gli 8 milioni di euro al Verona, se fosse andato al PSV e tu avessi voluto acquistarlo successivamente, magari dopo aver giocato in Champions League, il prezzo sarebbe salito a 30 o 40 milioni di euro. A volte bisogna accelerare un po' le cose. Ma all'interno del club lo conoscevano già tutti, e questo è stato positivo".

Sugli aspetti più sorprendenti di Cisse: "Tutto quello che riguarda la conduzione della palla, i cambi di direzione... Soprattutto quando era più giovane, riusciva a fare tantissima differenza in questo modo. Ma anche la capacità di utilizzare entrambi i piedi. È sempre più difficile trovare giocatori di questo tipo. È capace di giocare da esterno, ma anche da numero 10, da trequartista, come si dice in Italia, oppure da seconda punta. Quindi deve avere tante soluzioni a disposizione. E in Serie B gli avversari gli mettevano addosso una pressione enorme. Non è passato da un grande club europeo. All'inizio ha giocato in società più piccole. Ed è proprio questo, secondo me, che gli ha permesso di trovare soluzioni da solo e di crescere attraverso le difficoltà. Nessuno gli ha regalato niente, non ha avuto nulla di facile".

Amorim e la crescita di Cisse: "Se l'allenatore è in sintonia con il club e accetta di far giocare questi giovani, è perfetto per la società. A volte, come club, bisogna assolutamente dirsi: questo giocatore dobbiamo prenderlo. Poi vedremo cosa succederà, magari all'allenatore non piacerà, ma intanto avere quel giocatore sotto contratto, all'interno del club, è fondamentale. E bisogna vederlo il prima possibile. Amorim, quando era al Manchester United, aveva fatto una cosa interessante con Dorgu. Inizialmente lo impiegava come terzino sinistro, naturalmente, ma poi lo aveva schierato anche da ala destra in Premier League. Che abbia funzionato o meno, questo allenatore non ha avuto paura di fare scelte del genere e di cambiare posizione ai giocatori".

Come avete convinto il giocatore a scegliere il Milan invece del PSV? Gli avete promesso qualcosa di specifico? "Innanzitutto, noi non promettiamo niente. Penso che un club come il PSV Eindhoven possa dire a un giocatore: "Vieni qui e sarai titolare". Noi non potevamo dirglielo. La fortuna che abbiamo avuto è che, prima di tutto, ci chiamiamo AC Milan. E ti posso assicurare che questo rende le cose abbastanza facili. E soprattutto lui è italiano. Quindi, per un giocatore italiano, arrivare al Milan rappresenta una consacrazione. Per quanto il PSV Eindhoven possa promettergli di giocare titolare tutte le partite, resta comunque il PSV, nei Paesi Bassi. Non è l'AC Milan. Non ha lo stesso peso per la famiglia, per il giocatore e anche per il suo agente. Bisogna considerare tutte le persone che circondano il giocatore, è importante. E nel suo caso ho visto immediatamente che, appena ha sentito parlare del Milan, aveva già fatto la sua scelta. È stato positivo. Ho visto un ragazzo molto determinato. Sapeva già quello che voleva".

Oltre il talento, ci vuole anche un carattere forte: "A volte ci sono dei fenomeni, sai, giocatori talmente forti… Penso a Mbappé e Bernardo Silva, che erano con noi al Monaco. Erano talmente forti che quasi potevano permettersi di 'essere arroganti'. Anzi, a volte è persino meglio. Giocatori come Neymar o Rafa Leão hanno una grande 'arroganza calcistica'. Penso che Alphadjo non sia così. Ha questa umiltà perché viene da quella zona d'Italia, da Verona, perché è passato attraverso piccoli club. Penso che non si renda ancora conto del suo potenziale".

US Closes Loophole That Allowed China To Remotely Access Nvidia Chips

The Nvidia scandal fuels tougher China chip rules as Commerce targets Singapore and Malaysia

📅 Nov 16, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

When Jensen Huang resigned as Nvidia’s CEO on Thursday, critics of the U.S.’s policy of restricting chip sales to China lost one of their biggest allies.

In addition to being one of the industry’s biggest proponents of the AI buildout, Huang remains one of the most vocal critics of U.S. policies that bar the sale of advanced chips and chipmaking equipment to China. He claimed that these measures would encourage the creation of a competing Chinese chip industry, and that it was better for the U.S. to keep Chinese companies hooked on U.S. technology.

Nvidia’s CEO was also a strong supporter of open-source AI models—many of which came from Chinese developers like DeepSeek—in the face of criticism that such models posed a serious cybersecurity threat.

Revelations that Huang had an affair with Alice Huang (no relation)—a former executive at Megaspeed, a Singaporean company suspected of funnelling controlled chips to China—has torched the Nvidia CEO’s credibility on the issue of export controls. (Some reports claim U.S. intelligence considered Alice Huang to be part of a Chinese espionage campaign against Nvidia, but others have cast doubt on that assessment).

On Monday morning, the U.S. Department of Commerce released the first update to its export control regime since the scandal broke, issuing an interim final rule that closes a longstanding loophole that allowed Chinese companies to access AI chips based in overseas data centers, and expands export controls to an array of Southeast Asian countries. Congress, too, is debating several laws that would codify and expand existing export controls.

Yet tighter rules may also complicate matters for other countries trying to forge a more neutral path between the U.S. and China

Current export controls block the most advanced AI processors—made by companies like Nvidia, AMD, and Intel—from being sold to Chinese companies, though the Trump administration has allowed sales of Nvidia’s H200 to approved Chinese customers. Chipmaking equipment and high-bandwidth memory are also blocked.

Commerce’s new rule—which will go into effect immediately once it’s included in the Federal Register—would require licenses to export advanced AI chips to data centers in Indonesia, Malaysia, Singapore, Thailand, and Brazil. It would also require a license if large data center operators anywhere in the world want to grant access to companies headquartered in China, effectively closing the remote access loophole. Data centers already serving such companies will have 90 days from the rule’s effective date to get a license.

The Trump administration may remain split on how to act against chip smuggling; interestingly, the press release from the Commerce Department notes the rules were “written by and at the direction of the Department of Treasury and the National Security Council.” But Congress may act if the Trump administration remains divided.

“The regulatory path in Washington is going to be harder and bumpier,” Hanna Dohmen, a senior research analyst at Georgetown’s Center for Security and Emerging Technology, told Fortune before the rule’s release. She pointed out that several bills have been included in this year’s National Defense Authorization Act. The Huang scandal “adds momentum to push those bills through, and the chance of those passing are likelier than ever.”

“No matter what Trump does, it’s possible that Congress should be able to provide limitations,” Joshua Kurlantzick, a senior fellow at the Council on Foreign Relations, said before the rule’s release. “It’s not only about Trump anymore.”

Congress, meanwhile, is closer than ever to locking those controls into law.

The first bill, the Remote Access Security Act, also seeks to close the remote access loophole, giving the Commerce Department authority to license and penalize foreign companies tapping advanced U.S. AI chips from abroad. (The House passed the bill in January and is still awaiting consideration in the Senate).

The second bill, the Chip Security Act, would require advanced chips to ship with  security measures, like location tracking, to ensure processors aren’t diverted upon export. It also requires exporters to promptly disclose evidence of diversion or smuggling. Yet the act does not go as far as to mandate kill switches or more aggressive measures to stop smuggling.

The third, the AI Overwatch Act, would treat advanced chips like weapons: before any sale to China or other adversaries, the administration would have to notify Congress, which could then block the deal with a joint resolution of disapproval. It also carries an “America First AI” provision requiring Commerce to certify that exports won’t leave U.S. customers short of computing power.

The fourth, the MATCH Act, may be the most aggressive. The act would largely ban the sale and servicing of deep ultraviolet lithography tools across China, and imposes broader restrictions on facilities associated with major Chinese chipmakers like SMIC and CXMT. It gives allies like Japan and the Netherlands 150 days to align their own controls with Washington’s; if they fail, the U.S. can extend controls to equipment supplied from those countries, even if they contain no U.S.-made components.

Still, Dohmen pointed out that changing the rules alone won’t be enough to entirely halt chip smuggling. Several measures still require follow-on enforcement by either the U.S. or third parties; she pointed out that knowing that a chip was smuggled matters little if the Bureau of Industry and Security doesn’t act.

Much of the attention during the Nvidia scandal has fallen on Southeast Asia, and Singapore and Malaysia in particular. Megaspeed, the company accused of funnelling Nvidia chips to China, is based in Singapore; Malaysia was also reportedly the home of Megaspeed’s data centers. Megaspeed has denied sending chips to China.

“The administration wants to encourage Malaysia and Singapore to remain in the American AI ecosystem, but it doesn’t want them to become conduits through which Chinese firms can obtain advanced chips—or remotely access the computing power those chips provide,” Jennifer Lind, associate professor of government at Dartmouth College, said. “That has been its policy so far and I don’t anticipate this changing.”

The new Commerce Department rule does offer a carve-out for end-users that are, or are owned by, a U.S.-headquartered company. The region has attracted billions of dollars of investment from U.S. tech companies such as Microsoft, Oracle, and Amazon, which should qualify for that exemption, though the rule’s remote access limits still apply to them.

While both Singapore and Malaysia have made some public moves against chip smuggling, Kurlantzick said he’s “more inclined” to believe the Singaporean government was taking the matter seriously. Malaysia, in contrast, “wasn’t really doing anything, and didn’t care,” he said. “The U.S. is insanely unpopular in Malaysia.”

Stopping Chinese companies from accessing chips in overseas data centers could be a problem for countries like Malaysia, which want to become data center hubs. “For Malaysia, the controls could be disastrous,” Kurlantzick said. “From what Prime Minister Anwar Ibrahim sees, their biggest potential future industry is a data center and AI hub for Chinese companies.”

The U.S. is also trying to draw countries into its “Pax Silica” framework, an initiative to secure semiconductor and critical mineral supply chains that bypass China. Beijing has a competing framework, the World Artificial Intelligence Cooperation Organization; the U.S. is pushing countries to choose one or the other. (Trump is in Manila this week for the ASEAN Summit, where he’s expected to persuade Southeast Asian countries to join Pax Silica—and perhaps now answer some awkward questions about these expanded rules.)

Still, Kurlantzick thinks the U.S.’s lack of attention to the region will hold back efforts to get other countries on board. “The U.S. isn’t really offering Southeast Asian countries–or really anyone in Asia–much in return for their demands,” he said.

The biggest barrier to chips entering China right now isn’t Washington, but Beijing. The Chinese government has largely withheld permission for its companies to import Nvidia chips, instead pushing them to tap domestic chips made by companies like Huawei. DeepSeek in particular has led the drive to get Chinese AI models to run well on Huawei’s Ascend processors.

“Export controls still matter,” Lind said, though she admitted they may matter less now. “China is already well on its way toward developing its own AI ecosystem; Huawei makes its own chips for training and running AI models, and a software platform (CANN) that’s an alternative to Nvidia’s CUDA ecosystem,” she said.

She’s paying more attention to “whether semiconductor manufacturing equipment continues to be restricted—whether China develops the capability to manufacture its own frontier chips,” Lind said. “The Trump administration continues to restrict SME exports, and will likely continue to.”

Still, Huawei’s Ascend processors are not as good as the best Nvidia AI processors, particularly when it comes to training models.

Before the BIS announced its rule on Monday, Dohmen suggested that if Chinese companies can’t access Nvidia chips overseas, the roughly 600,000 H200s that Reuters reported are gathering dust in Hong Kong look more attractive.

“Would the Chinese government be more willing to let those H200s flow into China?” she asked. “If there is certain significant demand for remote access, and that’s cut off, there may actually be more need for the H200s.”

It’s not yet clear that China can supply all the computing power its AI developers need, particularly if overseas access disappears. China “does not have the capacity at this point to meet that demand,” Dohmen said.

Nicholas Gordon is an Asia editor based in Hong Kong, where he helps to drive Fortune’s coverage of Asian business and economics news.

Final Fantasy 14's ongoing pursuit in making its 500-hour story less daunting almost manifested into something in August, but Yoshi-P decided to postpone it

📅 Nov 16, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

I love Final Fantasy 14. I can also recognise that for anyone trying to start the game in the big 2026, it's incredibly daunting. Being faced with A Realm Reborn and five entire expansions' worth of story is a lot, and the question of how to funnel new players into Eorzea in a more approachable manner while still respecting the MMO's heavily narrative-driven vibe has been an ongoing debate for the last several years.

We know, at the very least, that director and producer Naoki Yoshida has been thinking about ways to make that barrier to entry a little less frightening—he said as much during Final Fantasy 14's Berlin Fan Festival—but according to a recent Letter from the Producer, it turns out we could've actually had something as recently as August.

"The idea of letting players freely choose a starting point for their adventure has been discussed, with the goal being to offer players a range of choices, rather than prescribe their experience according to my philosophy," Yoshida wrote.

In case you're not familiar with Yoshida's whole stance on this, he's long been staunchly protective of Final Fantasy 14's narrative integrity. Story skips are locked to the cash shop and, up until now, never included the current expansion. He's repeatedly expressed the fact that he's not too keen on letting people jump in wherever they feel, since it kinda mucks up the decade-plus worldbuilding and character relationships the game has built.

But, like many things he's been opposed to introducing in the game like unlocked glamour, he's letting up for the sake of allowing players more agency in how they engage with the game.

Anyway, back to letting people tackle its immense story a little easier. "With New Game+ working smoothly, this approach could work," Yoshida surmised. "In fact, we'd been laying groundwork to make this a possibility in conjunction with the release of the Nintendo Switch 2 version, but ultimately decided to postpone this initiative."

I do wonder specifically what made them stop—whether there was a hiccup somewhere, or whether Yoshida simply decided now wasn't the right time. He also expresses concern about a mismatch in player skill between those who jump ahead and those who've been around the block. "Even if you could start adventuring from a point of your choosing, the time and effort required to master a job remain a constant," he wrote. "We also need to avoid situations where both new and veteran players are left confused when matched together."

Keep up to date with the most important stories and the best deals, as picked by the PC Gamer team.

I don't want to be the one to tell Yoshi-P that the mismatch is still there even among players who've been around a long time, but I've seen an awful lot of omni-level characters who can't even do their basic rotation. So there's that.

"However, with all these considerations in mind, we've held meetings to discuss system and tutorial improvements, and have set ourselves a significant goal for the future. Although we'll need more time to realise this goal, I think giving players more freedom in their adventures will ultimately prove a positive change."

Honestly? I think even without story skips, tutorialisation and job mastery need to be better woven into the fabric of the game. Its low-level Hall of the Novice misses crucial info and includes odd holdovers from the days of things like the TP resource and much tougher aggro management—there's no emphasis on the importance of healers dealing damage, and tanks are told to only use their AOE for aggro generation before switching to single target.

It's all well and good trying to help players understand their role, but not when the information is outdated and fails to include key responsibilities. The tactical training exercises introduced early in Dawntrail are much better, and show how helpful such tutorials can be when they're properly updated and in line with over a decade's worth of in-game culture and community expectations.

Mollie spent her early childhood deeply invested in games like Killer Instinct, Toontown and Audition Online, which continue to form the pillars of her personality today. She joined PC Gamer in 2020 as a news writer and now lends her expertise to write a wealth of features, guides and reviews with a dash of chaos. She can often be found causing mischief in Final Fantasy 14, using those experiences to write neat things about her favourite MMO. When she's not staring at her bunny girl she can be found sweating out rhythm games, pretending to be good at fighting games or spending far too much money at her local arcade.

I’ve spent the last year testing out different cell phone plans, and Verizon's $30/month option certainly stood out, especially considering how it matched the $30/month rate I’ve been enjoying with Visible. While Big Red’s low-cost cell phone plan dishes up great savings, Red Pocket has one that’s way better, in my opinion, ahead of Black Friday — starting as low as $5 per month.

For a limited time, Red Pocket Mobile is offering its usual $10/month Essentials plan for just $5 per month. In order to get this deal, you need to select the option to pay annually with a one-time payment of $60 at checkout.

This basically comes out to a rate of $5 per month, which is unheard of considering that people still pay $80+ per month for their unlimited plans. Here’s what you get:

RedPocket Mobile: $5/month Essentials Plan @ RedPocket MobileFor a limited time, you can save big on your next cell phone plan thanks to RedPocket Mobile's Black Friday deals happening right now. When you choose the option to pay annually, its Essentials plan comes down to roughly $5 per month and includes unlimited talk and text, 3GB of premium data, international roaming, and more.

Red Pocket Mobile’s Essentials plan is one of the best cheap cell phone plans I’ve tested, offering customers unlimited talk and text, along with 3GB of premium data. It’s perfect for those who don’t want to spend a fortune, but still prioritize talk and text to stay connected.

While the 3GB of premium data might look laughable compared to the 500GB that Verizon offers with its Simplicity Plan, it’s ideal for those who primarily lean on staying connected via Wi-Fi.

On top of that, you get free or low-cost calling from the U.S. to over 20 international destinations and roaming in 60+ countries.

If you’re looking to get more data, you can get it with Red Pocket Mobile’s other cell phone plans. For about $13/month, the Plus plan ups your premium data to 20GB per month — while the Premium plan gives you a generous 50GB data allotment and 10GB of hotspot data for just $240 for the entire year, or roughly $20/month. Either way, these still save you substantial money on your wireless bill.

Did you know that in order to get the $30/month rate that Verizon’s advertising with its Simplicity Plan, you need to enable Auto Pay and port over an existing phone number? If you’re just setting up a new line, you lose the savings and end up paying $45/month for it — or an even heftier $55/month if you don’t enroll in Auto Pay and paper-free billing.

What I’m getting to here is that you don’t have to spend a lot to get a lot. For $5/month, it’s an all-you-can-eat helping of talk and text — while the 3GB of data is great to fall back on if you really need to check emails.
